# DONE — the record

Everything this project has finished, decided or ratified, one `- [x]` per item,
newest work appended at the end. **Nothing here is open and nothing here needs
reading front to back.** It is a grep target: when a fact comes from a record,
CLAUDE.md §1 says read forward to the end of that record, and one file makes
that one command.

```
grep -n "panel 062" docs/work/DONE.md      # what a sitting decided, and what overturned it
grep -n "f32" docs/work/DONE.md            # whether a question was already answered
```

**The record is append-only and is never rewritten** (CLAUDE.md §14). An item
that turns out to have been decided wrongly gets a *new* item saying so; the old
one keeps its words, because a record that edits its own past cannot be used to
check anything.

**How an item gets here.** A list holds only open items. The moment one is
ticked — by `/decide`, by `/step`, by a panel's ratification — it moves here with
the verdict written into it. That rule was written in three places and performed
by nobody until 2026-08-26: `docs/work/DECIDE.md` had grown to 391 KB holding 138
ticked items and zero open ones, and this file had not been touched in eight
days. The rule now lives in the skills that must obey it, which is the only place
a rule survives.

**The name was `QUEUE.md` until 2026-08-26.** It was renamed because it holds
finished work and its old name claimed the opposite, and because the reason given
for keeping that name — *"every commit subject in this repository cites this
path"* — was measured false: **24 subjects of 792, 3.0%**, the last of them 463
commits earlier, on 2026-08-14. Dated records (`DESIGN-LOG.md`, `docs/panel/`,
`docs/journal/`) keep the old spelling wherever they already used it, per §14.

Format, unchanged: `- [x] <origin> | <what> | <where to look> | <why it mattered>`

Twenty-three items near the top predate the checkbox convention and are dated
prose bullets from 2026-08-04. They are kept as written.

- [x] The record | author decision 2026-08-12 (2a) — **THE §11 SWEEP IS CLOSED, 2026-08-13.** Zero non-test files over ~300 lines, from 23 in breach when the decision was taken (26 counting the three the sweep itself pushed over). Twenty-two cut in one session, one commit each, every cut along a seam that names a concern. **The measurement that made "mechanical" a fact rather than a claim**: emitted C byte-identical over all 62 `run/` goldens at every single step — `adce2f13` from the first commit to the last — plus `heroes mutate` unchanged at 1255 mutants / 93% / 78%, 533 tests green, clippy clean, PORT-DEBT still 4, spec untouched at 2974. **Six defects were found by moving code that reading it had not found**: two docs describing rules the code no longer had (one of them one day old), a doc comment sitting on the wrong function, two parameters bound and discarded under prose saying they were used, a doubled computation shadowing itself, and a mutation operator with zero sites. **And one cost is on the record**: an index file grows by the act of tidying — `types/mod.rs` went 303 → 310 on `mod` lines alone before being cut to 224 | docs/debrief/DECIDE.md § the §11 sweep · CLAUDE.md §11 | a rule 23 files break is a preference, and it was the only thing watching the compiler's size after §1.6's global budget was refused

- [x] The record | panel 046, 2026-08-13 | **RATIFIED 2026-08-13 (a): R1–R5 as they stand, and (b) refused at its stated price.** The yes changed nothing in the resolution and settled three things the provisional default had left implicit, all three now in `docs/panel/046` § Ratification. **The rule is not retroactive**: R1 governs what is admissible as payment on the day of registration and cannot un-buy a clause already in the spec, so none of the six rows is invalidated and none of their clauses moves. **What R2 reaches is the looking, not the clause** — each of the six now has a milestone at which it is *re-decided*, and the date is written **once**, in `measure/gate.rs`'s module doc beside the rows, because six copies of one date is six places for it to rot. **The expiry is M-program-corpus and deliberately not M-selfhost-fixpoint**, which was the spec-warden's condition on its own yes: five of the six named metric 2, and setting the expiry at the instrument's arrival date would have made the expiry the tenancy. The sixth is the finding — `2675` named *"the next FFI rung"*, which closed 2026-08-12, **three days after the row was written and unlooked-at**: the compiler-engineer's evidence happening a third time in this repository, and the reason the date now lives beside the rows instead of in a panel file nobody greps. The warden's veto condition stays live and unmechanised — it fires if the first three `SPEC_TOKENS` rows after today name an instrument in name only, and (b) returns as the fallback with the freeze cost of R3. Was | **Ratify what a prediction buys** — provisional default adopted and landed: a registered prediction pays only if it names an instrument that **exists on the day of registration**, and an outstanding one is **re-decided, never renewed** (scored, or `lapsed` with its clause re-argued under the removal branch; never deleted). Refused: closing the branch until a metric-2 run exists — it freezes the spec at 2974 until v1, §1.0 compiler-need items included, which is the one cost the author may want to pay anyway. Three judges ran, not five and not the soundness lane; the file says what that gave up | docs/panel/046 · design.md §1.6 · .claude/skills/step/SKILL.md § 5 · crates/heroes/src/measure/gate.rs | six spec additions were bought with a promise nobody could collect, and the branch that could not pay was the cheap one

Verified 2026-08-12. - [x] The record | M4 robustness | **DEFECT, FIXED 2026-08-12 (panel 035 R7).** A `match` as the inline arm body of another `match` now compiles, runs and round-trips through `fmt`. The cause was one accidental production, not a grammar gap: after the inner `match` consumed its arms and its `Dedent`, the postfix loop read the *next outer arm's* leading `.` as a field access, so the error landed on a line the author had done nothing wrong on. **Five lines** in `syntax/expr.rs` — a suffix never reaches across a block that just closed — prototyped by the compiler-engineer, who counted the payoff: **45 sites** in the port write `=> match`, against 5 that would want the bracketed form, which is vetoed. Original: **DEFECT, open — diagnosed 2026-08-12, still unfixed.** The rejection is RIGHT: spec line 111 says an arm's body is one statement *inline* or an indented block, and a `match` whose arms sit below is neither — the legal spelling (`.ok v =>` then the inner `match` indented) compiles and runs. What is wrong is the message. Cause, traced: the inner `match` consumes its own arms and returns correctly, and then the **outer arm's statement keeps parsing** and swallows the next arm's leading-dot pattern, so the error lands on `_` of `.err _` — the next outer arm, a line the author did nothing wrong on. Fixing it is a parser continuation question, not a one-liner, and the author hit it first-hand writing a golden this session. Original entry: a `match` written as an **inline** arm body of another `match`, with its own arms indented below, is rejected — and the message points at the *next* outer arm ("expected the end of the line"). The same program in a block arm body is accepted. Reproducer in the queue entry below; §4.7 appears to allow it | tests/golden/ir/nested-match.hero (the comment) · syntax/control.rs arm() | a program that looks legal and is refused with a message about the wrong line is the failure mode this project spends tokens to avoid — and the parser is where it lives

- [x] Covered | panel 039 / DEFECT | **FIXED 2026-08-12 (author: option a — literals and other constants only).** `constant_body` refuses a call, a UFCS call, a `?` and a `???` in a `constant`'s body. The rule is **syntactic on purpose**: a purity analysis would admit `len("abc")` and refuse `len(args())`, which is a better rule and a whole new pass this tree does not have. The syntactic form buys the property outright — a call-free body reads only literals and other constants, and the cycle check proves those are not circular, so the value is a deterministic function of the program text and re-evaluation per read costs time and nothing else. Measured before implementing: **every** `constant` body in the repository is already a literal or arithmetic over constants, so the restriction broke zero programs. Two invariants fell out and both have tests: the **outermost** offender only (`len(args())` is two calls and one mistake), and the **cycle diagnostic is suppressed** where the body was already refused, because refusing the call does not remove the edge and reporting both is the two-for-one defect. - [x] found by the CI's first run | CI run 4, 2026-08-13 | **Does the FFI see the platform's headers as a POSIX system sees them, or strictly as C11? The two platforms disagree, and today the language's own promise is what breaks.** `tests/golden/run/ffi-constant.hero` binds `constant M_PI: f64` from `math.h`. On Darwin it compiles; on Linux the compiler says, correctly, `error[ffi_unknown_name]: `math.h` declares no `M_PI` — clang read the header and could not find it`. Neither side is wrong: **`M_PI` is not in C11** — it is POSIX/XSI — and glibc hides it under `-std=c11`, which defines `__STRICT_ANSI__`, while Darwin's `math.h` defines it unconditionally. CLAUDE.md §7 says the generated C is C11, and §4.19's whole promise is *"clang checks every signature and constant against that header, so a wrong FFI type is a compile error"*. Under strict C11 on glibc, a large part of what anybody actually binds — `M_PI`, `strdup`, `fileno`, `strtod_l` — is simply **invisible**, so the same `.hero` file compiles on one platform and not the other. That is the failure §4.19 exists to prevent, arriving through the flags rather than through the types. Three answers, and it is an architecture question (CLAUDE.md §4 — a panel path, and the panel does not block): (a) compile generated C as `-std=gnu11`, which is what every real C build does and what the headers are written for, at the cost of §7's sentence; (b) keep `-std=c11` and have the emitter define `_DEFAULT_SOURCE`, which is narrower and still a deliberate departure from the standard the project names; (c) keep strict C11 and accept that the FFI can only reach ISO C, which makes §1.11's *"everything comes from C libraries"* false in a way the SQLite acceptance test happens not to notice. **Meanwhile the golden is red on Linux and green on Darwin, and that is left visible rather than papered over** | tests/golden/run/ffi-constant.hero · crates/heroes-cli/src/commands/toolchain.rs (FLAGS) · CLAUDE.md §7 · design.md §4.19, §1.11 | the one promise the C backend exists to keep is the one that does not survive a second platform **CLOSED 2026-08-14 by panel 047, ratified.** Answer (a): `toolchain.rs` names `-std=gnu11`, and the ratification's content was that the dialect is *named* rather than which one won. `gnu11` over `c11` followed from the measurement — one predefined macro, `__STRICT_ANSI__`, identical IR. Two more platforms have since reproduced the class from their own direction: MSVC hides the same constants behind `_USE_MATH_DEFINES`, which is now named in `FLAGS` beside it.

- [x] found by the CI's first run | CI runs 3 and 4, 2026-08-13 | **`tests/golden/run/abort-slice-splits-a-character.hero` failed once on Linux at `-O2` and passed on the next run with nothing relevant changed.** Its stdout was empty where `caff\n` was expected — a program that prints, then aborts, having printed nothing. `hero_panic` calls `fflush(stdout)` before `abort()` and `heroes run` inherits stdout rather than capturing it, so neither of the two obvious explanations fits. It has not reproduced since, and the harness now reports the exit status and stderr on a mismatch, so the next occurrence will say more than this one could. Decide whether to chase it now or wait for it: a flake in the abort path is the class this language cannot afford to have — the whole of §4.14 is that a program which must stop, stops visibly | tests/golden/run/abort-slice-splits-a-character.hero · runtime/parts/panic.c · crates/heroes-cli/tests/expectation/mod.rs | an intermittent failure in the mechanism that makes aborts observable, seen once and not since **ANSWERED 2026-08-14 (`b` — chased, and it has a cause).** Three things were checked. (1) **Every abort path flushes**: `panic.c`, `failure.c` ×3 and `alloc.c` all `fflush(stdout)` before writing to stderr and calling `abort()`, so the buffering hypothesis is out. (2) **Not reproducible here**: 1000 runs of the `-O2` binary on Darwin — 400 through a pipe, 300 through a file, 300 across eight parallel workers — gave an empty stdout **zero** times. (3) **The timestamps explain it.** Compile-beside-and-rename for the *binary* landed in `5ecbc98` at **08-13 23:32**; the seven CI runs of 2026-08-13 all ran between **22:54 and 23:28**, so runs 3 and 4 executed on a tree that still wrote the binary **in place** — while `cargo test` runs its binaries in parallel. One process truncating a binary another is about to exec produces a program that starts and prints nothing, which is the symptom exactly, and the `-O2` leg has its own cache path and so its own race window. It has not recurred in any run since 23:32, which is consistent. **Not marked as a fixed defect with a case of its own**, because a test for two processes racing on one path is not deterministic and the fix it would pin is already asserted by the `publish` helper's own doc — but if it recurs after 23:32 this diagnosis is wrong and the item reopens with that fact.

- [x] found by the CI's first run | CI run 1, 2026-08-13 | **The Cyclone rule's own enforcement now fires on `vec!`, and the toolchain is pinned while you decide which way to fix it.** The first CI run ever to compare two machines found clippy **1.97** rejecting **forty** lines that clippy **1.90** accepts, on both platforms identically — so it is the toolchain and not the platform. Every one is `vec![…]`, every message is *use of a disallowed type `std::boxed::Box`*, and this project has **zero** `Box` in its own Rust: `vec!` expands to `Box::new([…])` inside the standard library and the newer lint reads into that expansion. The rule `clippy.toml` states is *"Heroes' only indirection is the array — use Vec or indices"*, so the lint is now refusing the macro that builds the sanctioned indirection. **Landed meanwhile**: `rust-toolchain.toml` pins 1.90.0, written once and honoured by `rustup` for local and CI alike, which is a fix a compiler with a byte-identical fixpoint as its acceptance criterion wanted anyway. **What is open is which way it comes off the pin**: (a) exempt `vec!` — but `disallowed-types` has no macro knob, so this means `#[allow]` at forty sites or dropping `Box` from the list, and dropping it stops catching a real `Box` the day somebody writes one; (b) stay pinned and move the version deliberately, which is defensible for this project and makes the pin a thing somebody must remember; (c) check first whether 1.97's reach is a clippy **regression** — most lints skip external macro expansions — in which case it is reported upstream and (b) holds until it is fixed | clippy.toml · rust-toolchain.toml · `.github/workflows/ci.yml` · CLAUDE.md §5, §10 | the tool that enforces the Cyclone rule is now wrong about the Cyclone rule, and a pinned toolchain is a decision nobody took **ANSWERED 2026-08-14 (`c`, then `a`).** The upstream search was run and found **no report of this class**, but it found the reason it may not be one: current `alloc` builds `vec!` through `Box` on purpose (`write_box_via_move`, to keep unoptimised builds off the stack), so the lint is reading a real `Box` rather than misreading a macro. That makes it **the project's rule that is too broad**, not clippy that is wrong — `disallowed-types` is meant to catch the *author's* `Box`, and it cannot tell one from `alloc`'s. So (b) is not available cheaply and (c) returns nothing: the pin stays, and the version moves deliberately. What is left open, and is smaller: whether `Box` should come off `disallowed-types` altogether, since it is now catching a thing the Cyclone rule sanctions and cannot catch the thing it was written for without also catching that.

- [x] panel 046 | `docs/measurements/007`, 2026-08-13 | **A reader told a loop is quadratic, and given no non-loop, does the loop.** Panel 037's +39 bought a spec sentence naming both `+`'s copy and `push`'s. A blind reader avoided `+` *"for one reason — that sentence … is the only sentence in the document with a cost claim in it, so it is loud"* and then wrote `push`-in-a-loop in two programs of three, noticing only afterwards: *"the warning redirected me from one quadratic accumulation to the other."* The cause is not the wording. It is that **`map` cannot capture a local** (no lambda, no partial application) and there is **no `repeat`**, so the two programs that needed a captured separator and N copies of one character had no other route — while the third, whose transform captures nothing, is the one line with no accumulator in it. Decide which of the three this is: a Part 7 lambda question, a `repeat` built-in (one name, no new type, and §1.0 will want it for the port's own error rendering), or a spec that stops claiming a cost it leaves no way to avoid | docs/measurements/007 · docs/panel/037 § Ratification · spec § Strings, arrays, maps | the clause's first half held under a real reader and its second half is falsified, and the falsifier is a language gap rather than a sentence **ANSWERED 2026-08-14 (`a`), and panel 054 settled the shape.** `repeat(s: str, n: u64)` lands at +14 against a −5 removal the sitting found, net +9; the runtime primitive is 13 lines. The count is a **`u64`** because design.md §1.12 chose among three answers to the negative case — a defensive check must **surface** a defect, not hide it — so a negative count does not compile and a computed one goes through `to_u64().must()`, which aborts at the subtraction. The multiply is guarded by a division: `4 * (2^62+2)` wraps *positive* to 8 and was **exit 138, SIGBUS, silent** before the guard, the class Rust shipped as CVE-2018-1000810. And the clause itself is repaired rather than deleted, on the rule the sitting adopted: a cost claim is admissible only where the same paragraph names a construct the reader can reach for. **`repeat` is recorded as *not* compiler-need** — the port's own renderer builds its padding as a tab-preserving per-character map, not `repeat(" ", col)`. The lambda half stays refused (CLAUDE.md §13, Part 7).

- [x] **RATIFIED 2026-08-13: the uniform `T?` is restored and step 7's bend is reverted** (*"fai la soluzione più solida e sicura"*). One rule instead of two, no invention — the historian found the shipped encoding search-negative across five languages while Zig's `math.cast`, `fit`'s exact shape, returns `?T` unconditionally — and taken at the cheap moment: 8 sites today against ≥25 at M-selfhost-probe, which scores the compiler-engineer's prediction 4 inside its own "still cheaply reversible" branch. Was | **RATIFICATION STILL PENDING, and panel 043 sharpened it rather than closing it** (2026-08-12). The implementation survived a real attack — all 64 pairs generated from an *independent reimplementation*, zero errors; C's usual-arithmetic conversions shown structurally unable to bite; reverting measured at **9 lines and 8 sites**. **The proposal's central claim was wrong and the compiler-engineer corrected it**: panel 042's Q2 chose among *names* and **never ruled on a widening**, so this bends the **author's** ruling and nobody else's. Its load-bearing condition is now met — a `certain` fix removing a `.must()` that cannot fail, and a `guess` adding one where a `T?` met a `T` — which is what turns the extra locality hop into a machine-applied repair instead of a round-trip. **And the historian found the option that was never on your menu**, the only one with a shipped precedent (Rust's): two names, both always available — `fit_i64(aU8) -> i64` a *compile error* on a lossy pair with a certain fix naming the other form, and `try_fit_u8(anI64) -> u8?` for every pair. It kills the lexer's `.must()` too, keeps the return type a function of the **name**, and costs eight more names. Heroes' rule itself is D's `to!T` and Rust's `From`/`TryFrom` partition; only the *encoding* — one name, two shapes — was search-negative across five languages. So the choice is: keep what shipped, or take the third option. Was | **A widening conversion returns `T`, not `T?` — which bends your ruling 3, and the reason is measured rather than argued.** You ruled that conversions return `T?`; applied uniformly, `s[i] -> u8` put this on the calculator's lexer: `v @ v * 10 + fit_i64(l.here() - '0').must()` — a `.must()` on a conversion that **cannot fail**, at the hottest line of the shape the closure list is made of, and `is_digit`/`digit_of` each carried one too. So `fit_<w>` now returns `T` where `IntKind::contains` says the source width fits the target and `T?` where it does not. **What this does NOT bend is panel 042's Q2**, which refused one name with two result types on the ground of *unpredictability*: this is a function of the argument's width, the compiler knows it, and it is fallible exactly when it can fail — which is also what the llm-ergonomist asked for and was overruled on, for uniformity, three steps before the measurement went its way. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **11 of 23**: `emit/gate.rs` **372 → 146**, into the two tables its own doc had already named — `gate_types.rs` 142 (what the runtime can represent: the table that is *empty of refusals* today, and whose descent into element types is a filed defect rather than tidiness) and `gate_ops.rs` 133 (what the emitter can write, gating by name as well as by op because `xs.len()` and `for` lower differently). What stayed is neither table: the **list** — one entry per capability, deduplicated on the message, sorted by first appearance. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **12 of 23**: `mutate/edits.rs` **367 → 214**, cut by *what mistake is being imitated* rather than by size — `edits_typo.rs` 180 holds the three operators where the mutation is **identical** and only the target changes, which is what turns three operators into one measurement: an identifier (the resolver catches it, 100%), an error code (**nothing** catches it, 0% — the one operator of eleven that points at no rule which kills it), a constant's digit. Verified by the instrument itself: `heroes mutate` produces **1255 mutants, 93% / 78%**, the same corpus and the same rates as before the cut. **Found while verifying, and it is a real gap**: `typo-digit` has **0 sites** on the whole corpus — an operator that measures nothing, which is §9's *"every verifier check has a test that makes it fire"* in mutation form. Not fixed here; it is not a sweep's business. **13 of 23**: `source/mod.rs` **340 → 182**, cut on *when* the code runs — `build.rs` 95 is everything that happens **once, before any offset exists** (`InputFile`, and the three ways several files become one text), `locate.rs` 92 is from an offset to a place a person reads (line, column, the text a span covers — every caret and every `#line`), and `mod.rs` keeps the questions *about* an offset that already exists: which file, which module, is it the library, is it the root. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **14 of 23**: `emit/ctype.rs` **338 → 131**, one cut between two jobs with different failure modes — `ctype.rs` says what a type *is* in C (a wrong answer is a clang error), `typedefs.rs` 237 says what it is *named* (a wrong answer is a **collision**, which clang reports about a line nobody wrote). The leading-digit rule that keeps the emitter's invented names apart from the author's moves with the table it protects. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **15 of 23**: `types/stmts.rs` **323 → 260**, and this one moved *arms* rather than a section — `jumps.rs` 122 takes the four whose answer to `Flow` is the file's own reason to exist: `return` and `break`/`continue` **jump**, `while` and `for` **fall through always**, because a loop may run zero times. That last line is why a function whose only `return` sits inside a loop is still `missing_return`, and it was one comment inside a 323-line file. The match keeps every arm named. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **16 of 23**: `types/builtins.rs` **322 → 260** — `fallible_ops.rs` 98 takes `must`, `default` and `is_err`, the three whose whole rule is the same one (*the argument must be fallible*), together with the diagnostic for when it is not, because that is the answer all three give to the same mistake. **And it caught a doc that had outlived its rule by one day**: `unwrapping_nothing`'s comment said *"a widening returns `T` rather than `T?`, so a reader carrying the uniform habit writes a `.must()` that has nothing to unwrap"* — the 2026-08-13 ratification restored the uniform `T?` and deleted that source of the mistake, while the `certain` fix it justifies is worth **more** now, not less. Rewritten to say what is true. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **17 of 23**: `heroes-cli/src/commands/compile.rs` **317 → 262** — `contract.rs` 80 is not pipeline at all, it is **how the command talks**: CLAUDE.md §10's one sentence (artifact on stdout, diagnostics on stderr, 0/1/2) and the two consequences that are easy to lose among the compiler passes — a flag that asked two questions is an error rather than an answer to one of them (`-O0 -O2`, the same reading `build` gives `--dump-ir --emit-c`), and `build`/`run` take no diagnostic-shaping flags because `check` already answers that. The backend half of the pipeline was the other candidate and was **rejected**: extracting it needed eight parameters, which is a worse file than the one it fixes. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **18 of 23**: `resolve/exprs.rs` **313 → 263** — `places.rs` 68 is the left of `@`, and the concern is that **resolving a name there is not reading it**: in `v @ v + 1` the root is *written* and the `i` of `xs[i] @ …` is *read*. That distinction is what makes spec line 87's *"a read is a use and a write is not"* implementable at all, and it was one comment inside the file that treats every other occurrence as a read. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **19 of 23**: `types/sized.rs` **312 → 243** — `sized_cycle.rs` 91 is what the reader is *shown* about a cycle, and it separates from finding one because it is bound by rules the walk is not: the caret lands on the **field** (Swift's shape, chosen over rustc's), the cycle is **rotated to the lowest declaration index** so reordering two unrelated declarations does not rewrite a golden, and the fix is a **`guess`** because `[T]` and `{K: V}` both break the cycle and the compiler cannot know which was meant. This is the file whose ≤150-line prediction panel 023 scored as **falsified** at 298; it is now 243 + 91. **AND THE LIST IS 8, NOT 5** — re-measured: the sweep has been pushing `types/mod.rs` **up** (303 → 309), because every split adds a `mod` line to it. An index file grows by the act of tidying, and that has to be said rather than discovered at the end. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **20 of 23**: `types/mod.rs` **310 → 224** — `checked.rs` 112 is what the checker leaves behind, **the same seam `resolve/resolved.rs` was cut on an hour earlier**, and that is the finding: two sibling passes had the same shape, each holding *how the answer is computed* and *what the answer is*, with only the second read by anyone else. Which also means the cut that fixes an index file is not to shorten the index — it is to move what is not index out from under it. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **21 of 23**: `own.rs` **309 → 242** — `owning.rs` 87 answers *what needs a reference at all*, where `own.rs` answers *where the incref and the release go*. It earns a file because it is the question this pass gets wrong **silently**: every other mistake in the ownership pass is a crash or a sanitiser report, and a wrong answer here is a **leak**, which nothing in an ordinary build reports — it took `hero_runtime_check_leaks()` saying *3 heap blocks still live at exit* to find the first one. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **22 of 23**: `ir/inst.rs` **308 → 254** — `ids.rs` 75 is how the IR *names* things, and it separates from what an instruction *is* because everything in it follows from a rule that has nothing to do with instructions: **no struct may hold a reference** (the Cyclone rule), so an id is a `u32` index and anything variable-length is a **run**. That is what keeps `Op` `Copy` even though `Call` and `Construct` are n-ary. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **23 of 23**: `syntax/stmt.rs` **306 → 243** — `name_stmt.rs` 94 is the difficulty the file's own doc opens with: **three line shapes begin with a name**, and one token of lookahead tells them apart. The reason the type is mandatory on `v: i64 @ 0` lives there now, next to the code that depends on it — without it `v @ 0` and `v @ v + 1` are the same shape, and whether a name is being declared or written would depend on a declaration elsewhere in the file, which is §1.3's locality test failing. C byte-identical, 533 green, clippy clean. **The original 23 are done.** Then, on the re-measured list: **`emit/externs.rs` 306 → 90**, with `extern_assert.rs` 250 taking the half that **checks** what the head line says against the real header. It separates on more than size: it is the only place in the backend where a clang failure is the **author's** mistake rather than the compiler's — exit 1 and a `ffi_return_type` diagnostic on the `.hero` line, not exit 2 and *"the compiler is wrong"* (CLAUDE.md §7's one named exception). Then **`lexer/scan.rs` 304 → 197**, with `literals.rs` 130 taking the two scanners whose content is a value the author wrote out. They belong together because they share the rule panel 008 spent a sitting on — **the backslash is reserved**, five escapes, any other escape a compile error — and because both hold the recovery rule that a bad escape does **not** poison the token: one mistake inside a string costs one diagnostic instead of derailing the line. And last, **`types/exprs.rs` 301 → 241**, with `contextual.rs` 75 holding §4.5's ⇐-only list — *is this form one with no type of its own?* — in one place, so that two readings of it cannot become two languages. **THE SWEEP IS CLOSED**: `find crates -name '*.rs' -not -path '*/tests/*' | xargs wc -l | sort -rn` has **no non-test file over 300** (`modules/tests.rs` 583 is a test file and outside §11's words, as this item said when it opened). Twenty-two files cut in one session on top of the three before it, 195 non-test files now, C emitted **byte-identical on every single one** (`adce2f13` unchanged from the first cut to the last), `heroes mutate` still 1255 / 93% / 78%, PORT-DEBT still 4, spec untouched at 2974: `emit/inst.rs` (445) is a **single exhaustive `match` over `Op`** whose whole purpose is that a new IR instruction cannot be added without this file and `ir/print_inst.rs` both failing to compile. So the cut must move arm *bodies* out and leave every arm in place — the candidates are the two `Abort` arms (what the author sees when a program stops), the five `Construct` shapes, and the access arms (`Tag`, `Payload`, `Field`, `Index`, `Len`). A cut that collapses arms into a `_ =>` would trade §11 for the property §11 exists to protect, and CLAUDE.md §11's own loud-fallback rule forbids it. Tick each file here as it lands, with its before → after | CLAUDE.md §11 · `find crates -name '*.rs' -not -path '*/tests/*' \| xargs wc -l \| sort -rn` | the sweep's own risk is that it rewrites the compiler while nothing else is being watched, so one file per commit is the whole safety mechanism

(ii) Q1's **value** reading is **held, not adopted** — 4 of 5 judges would take it, but the spec-warden's consistency argument is unanswered: panel 035's R6 refused exponents for *"a new syntactic form for a type not on the closure list"*, and **the closure list is a list of forms, not of types**, so `int`'s presence on it carries hex no more than `f64`'s carried exponents. Adoption costs a tightened **+42** measured and **is conditional on the −39 removal landing in the same commit** (spec lines 152–153, the string-building performance advice, which CLAUDE.md §13 makes a non-justification). (iii) `u64`-at-the-boundary is **vetoed on a measured inversion**: `extern constant SIZE_MAX: u64` + `to_i64` is a binary that **traps** where today it is a compile error. (iv) full sized integers and first-class `u64` carry **two vetoes each**. (v) `e6-cheapest.c` — one extra pair of generic associations in the existing `_Generic`, binding `strlen`, `SIZE_MAX` and `CURLAUTH_ANY` with **no new type, no spec token, no ABI bump** and 6/6 wrong signatures still errors — is **recorded and not adopted**, because the historian pre-refuted it without knowing it existed: `dart:ffi` is Heroes' exact integer model, shipped the marker shape ~7 years ago, and its users filed *"nobody told us the value went negative"*; the widened assertion has **no marker at all**. Taking it compels a written **LP64-only premise with the test that fires when it dies** (`-target i386-linux-gnu`) plus a `sizeof(...)==8` companion assertion, and an amendment to design.md §4.19's two sentences, which currently book the refusal as an achievement. (vi) What ships at **zero spec tokens with no judge against it**: a `hex_literal` diagnostic, because `x = 0xFFFF` says *"expected the end of the line, found a name (`xFFFF`)"* — a message about a name, for a number. (vii) The **spec's FFI promise is false** and no option on the menu repairs it: *"clang checks every signature and constant against that header, so a wrong FFI type is a compile error"* is silently untrue at the integer boundary, and the llm-ergonomist wrote `strlen(s: cstr) -> int` in two variants **knowing it was wrong, because nothing else could be written and nothing would complain** | docs/panel/041-notation-and-the-unsigned-boundary.md · design.md Part 7 item 10, §4.19 · spec § Types, § FFI | the four judges who can see the repository rejected the mechanism; not one of them disputed the gap the judge reading only the spec measured

The brief carries both, and the ticked item above states what the pairing costs | **Are the base types wrong — does Heroes need sized or unsigned integers (`u64`, `i64`, …)?** Asked by the author while reading panel 039's findings, and it is the largest open language question. **The evidence already exists and is compiled**: an ffi-pragmatist found two real header constants Heroes cannot express — `CURLAUTH_ANY` = `(~((unsigned long)1<<4))` and `SIZE_MAX` = `((size_t)-1)` — and classified them itself as *"a type-vocabulary gap (Part 7 item 10), not an evaluation gap"*. **Two citations in this item were wrong and are corrected here** (verified 2026-08-12, third `/decide` session): the two constants are in **`docs/panel/040-the-bitwise-set.md` § finding 4 and prediction 3**, not in panel 039 — 039 contains neither name — and they are not `ffi_constant_type` diagnostics: that code exists and fires (`types/decls.rs:244`), but nothing has been written that makes these two produce it, so what the record holds is a judge's compiled reading, not a diagnostic transcript. **Part 7 item 10 mis-locates the pressure**, which is the finding to open with: it defers sized integers as *"needed for any binary format"*, when the live case is the **FFI**, which is §1.11's founding constraint and is inside v1 — the same shape as the `Macros` row panel 039 repaired, a correct decision filed under a reason that does not describe the real case. Note that **the bitwise set does not close this**, and the reason is not the one panel 040 wrote: `0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF` is **not** `int_out_of_range`, because **Heroes has no hexadecimal literal at all** — the lexer scans `0`, then `xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF` as a name, and the program dies with `expected_end_of_line` at column 10 (measured 2026-08-12; zero occurrences of hex scanning in `lexer/`, zero mentions of `0x` in the spec). The decimal form is the one that reaches the type checker: `18446744073709551615` → `int_out_of_range`, with the note naming the signed range. Two facts, both measured on this machine after the fact. (1) `pkg-config --libs sdl2` answers `-lSDL2main -lSDL2 -Wl,-framework,Cocoa`, and **`SDL2main` replaces the program's `main`** with SDL's own, which initialises Cocoa — so any Heroes program that links it becomes a windowed application. Eleven corpus programs in three configurations each filled the author's dock with app icons. (2) This machine's `libSDL2` is **`sdl2-compat 2.32.70`**, a shim that loads **SDL3 at runtime** and, when it cannot, opens a **modal dialog** — *"Fatal error! Cannot continue! On an unattended test run that is not a failure, it is a **hang**: two processes sat at 0% CPU for ninety minutes until they were killed by hand. `examples/sdl/` was removed for this reason; the binding itself is correct and `heroes run` on it prints what it should. **What is yours**: whether the allow-list's property test needs a second half. Today it asks *does this word name a file, load anything, or write anything* — and `-lSDL2main` does none of those and still **replaces the program's entry point**, which no other library on the list can do. It is not build-time code execution, which is what CVE-2018-6574 was about, so panel 050's argument does not reach it; it is a runtime shape change that the corpus cannot survive. Options as they look from here: leave it (the author of a program that links SDL knows what SDL is), refuse `*main` by name (narrow, and a premise about naming conventions), or say nothing in the list and instead give the **corpus** a timeout, which catches this and every future hang | docs/panel/055 · crates/heroes-cli/src/commands/libraries.rs · crates/heroes-cli/tests/corpus.rs | the allow-list was measured against what a flag can do at build time, and this is what a library can do at run time. **Two things landed the same evening and neither closes this question.** (a) The corpus has a **timeout** — two minutes, chosen against the slowest thing it actually does, so it is the line between *slow* and *never* rather than a performance budget (§13). (b) `-Wl,-rpath,<dir>` joined the allow-list, because SDL3's own `.pc` answers with it and `package "sdl3"` was refused for a flag that names a directory — **and it is the second instance of this very question in one hour**: an rpath names no file, loads nothing and writes nothing at build time, and changes what the loader picks at run time. Its escalation is written in the code: a hostile `.pc` saying `-L/tmp/x -lfoo` already chooses the library, so the rpath is not a new capability, but it lets the choice be **swapped after the build** by anyone who can write to that directory. The example itself is now **SDL3**, which has no `SDL2main` and no compat shim, and the corpus runs it in 30 seconds

- [x] Owed a panel — the path pair | **CLOSED 2026-08-15 by `docs/panel/057` — refused, three vetoes, and the
  proposal that lost was the one written in this file.** `path_join` fails §1.0 on
  its own concession; `path_parent`'s §1.0 claim was `directory_text` and **it does
  not compute it**, measured twice. Nothing lands: the port writes the scan once in
  `modules/` at 0 spec tokens, which was option (c) all along. The `\`-as-separator
  clause is refused outright with a falsifier that already fired. The Tier-2 route
  is measured at zero compiler lines and left available. **The ratification item is
  below.**

**This is the one item of the eight `/decide` took on 2026-08-15 that `/decide`
could not close, and the reason is jurisdictional rather than a hesitation.** Two
new built-in names plus spec tokens is surface, so CLAUDE.md §4 makes it a `/panel`
path, and the soundness lane does not fit because the change costs spec tokens. The
skill's own rule is to say so and stop. **The proposal is already instructed, so the
sitting can be convened whenever there is time to wait for it:**

> **Adopt `path_join(dir: str, name: str) -> str` and `path_parent(p: str) -> str?`,
> neither of which normalises.**
>
> - **`path_parent` is the one with the §1.0 argument.** The port's module loader
>   must take a path the author typed and find its directory part;
>   `modules/mod.rs`'s `directory_text` does `path.rfind(['/', '\\'])`, looking for
>   **either** separator because the author may have typed a Windows one. In Heroes
>   that is a backwards loop over a string looking for two characters — the shape a
>   built-in exists to remove.
> - **`path_join` has no §1.0 argument and comes along anyway.** Verified: no corpus
>   program builds a path, and `dir + "/" + name` is already correct on all three
>   platforms, so it buys **discoverability, not correctness**. It enters as the
>   inverse of a function that does have the argument, or not at all.
> - **`path_parent` returns `str?`.** Absent when there is no separator. Returning
>   `"."` would be inventing a meaning; `str?` makes the caller handle it and is the
>   language's own idiom (§4.6, absence as a variant).
> - **`path_join` inserts one `/` unless `dir` already ends in `/` or `\`** — a fact
>   about the value in hand (§11), not a decision about a path's meaning.
> - **Neither normalises, and that is the load-bearing half.** The runtime is
>   portable today *because it does nothing to a path*: `runtime/parts/os.c` has no
>   `strcat`, no separator literal, no normalisation, and the string reaches `fopen`
>   verbatim. A `path_join` that rewrote `\` to `/` or collapsed `..` would be the
>   first place this project decides what a path **means**, and that is a larger
>   step than the name suggests — it is also the step that has produced security
>   defects in every language that took it eagerly.
>
> **What the sitting must price**: the spec tokens for two names against §1.6's
> headroom (888 today), and whether `path_parent` alone is the honest §1.0 purchase
> with `path_join` refused under Principle 0's *neither → it waits*.

Was | author instruction, 2026-08-14 | **A runtime function that joins two path pieces — and the honest question is whether joining is the half that is missing.** Verified before writing this: **no program in the corpus builds a path today**, `runtime/parts/os.c` does nothing to one (no `strcat`, no separator literal, no normalisation — the string reaches `fopen` verbatim), and `/` works on all three platforms because Windows accepts it in every filesystem API. `examples/adventure/main.hero` proves it on the Windows leg with a literal `examples/adventure/walkthrough.txt`. So a program that wants to join **can** today: `dir + "/" + name` is correct everywhere, and a doubled separator from a trailing `/` is harmless on all three. **Which means `path_join` buys discoverability, not correctness — and that is not a Principle 0 argument.** What *is* one is the other half. The port's module loader must take a path the **author typed** on the command line and find its directory part: `crates/heroes/src/modules/mod.rs`'s `directory_text` does `path.rfind(['/', '\\'])`, looking for **either** separator, because the author may have typed a Windows one. In Heroes today that is writable — `chars`, `slice`, `len` — and it is a loop over the string backwards looking for two characters, which is the shape a built-in exists to remove. **So the three options are not join-or-nothing**: (a) `path_join(dir, name)` alone, which is the asked-for thing and the half the port does not need; (b) **the pair** — `path_join` and `path_parent` — where the second is the one with the §1.0 argument and the first comes along because a program that can take a path apart will want to put one together; (c) neither, and the port writes the backwards loop once, in the file that already knows why (`modules/`), with `directory_text`'s own comment carried over. **A fourth thing to decide with it**: whether either function normalises. The runtime's portability today is that it does **nothing**, written down as a claim that can die — a `path_join` that rewrote `\` to `/`, or collapsed `..`, would be the first place this project makes a decision about a path's *meaning*, and that is a larger step than the name suggests | runtime/parts/os.c · crates/heroes/src/modules/mod.rs (`directory_text`) · crates/heroes/src/library/source.hero · spec § Strings, arrays, maps | the runtime is portable because it does nothing to a path, and the first function that does something is where that stops being true

Two halves, decided differently. **The message is repaired now**: it said *"C would write through it"*, which is **false on the first function anyone tries** — `free` deallocates, `putenv` retains — so it now says *"C does not promise to leave it alone"*, which is what a header actually declares. Only the header knows which permission is exercised, and asserting a mechanism where only a permission is known is CLAUDE.md §11 pointed at prose. **The leak is recorded as design.md Part 8 wart 18 with the §12 falsifier**, not fixed: §4.19 already names an ownership vocabulary as future work, and the judges' position was that this is the program making it concrete rather than a defect to patch now. Falsifier: a binding on §4.19's ladder or the closure list that must free what C allocated; measured today, there is none. Was | panel 059's historian, sourced 2026-08-15 | **Nullability belongs to the declaration, and four systems say so independently — it was on nobody's ballot.** Clang's `_Nonnull`/`_Nullable` with audited regions (r240156, 2015); Swift SE-0055 making `UnsafePointer<Int>?` the nullable form and mapping `_Nullable` → `Optional` at import (Swift 3); Zig spelling it in the **return type**, `std.posix.getenv(key) ?[:0]const u8`; Ada's `Null_Ptr` as a *value of the pointer type* since 1995, with `Value` raising precisely because the programmer could have tested first. Every abort-camp precedent pairs the abort with a testable null — which Heroes has, verified during the sitting. **Not a proposal**: Principle 0 does not fund it and no program needs it today. Filed so a future sitting on the C boundary starts from this shape instead of rediscovering it, and so the record shows it was seen and not taken | docs/panel/059 § Findings · design.md §4.19 | the best findings in this project have been the option that was never on the ballot, and this one arrived after the vote rather than before it

The compiler-engineer priced it as **a second kind of type** rather than a flag on a declaration: `emit/perfn.rs:60-61` emits `_eq`/`_hash` for every aggregate unconditionally and `descriptors.rs:270` puts `_hash` in the `HeroDesc`, so a partial record needs a second arm in `perfn::every_generated_type`, a second table in `descriptors::generated`, and a refusal at every container site — and a dropped `hash` is the null descriptor panel 022 measured as `SEGV on unknown address 0x0, pc 0x0`, no type name and no line. The provisional default takes **complete first because it is the reversible order**. Every other file in the directory is cycle-free and ports as its own module. **Nothing is blocked**: mutual recursion inside one module is free, so each knot ports as one long Heroes module and the grammar works. **What is owed is a rule.** The port proposes: the language rule stands (panel 031 R6 chose refusal as the reversible direction, and relaxing it spends the topological order M-separate-compilation needs — the trade 031 already priced), and **§11 yields for a grammar knot with its seam named**, exactly as it yielded for `toolchain.rs` at 400 on 2026-08-14 — where the accepted reason was that the cut ran through a seam, and here the seam is *forbidden by the language*, which is stronger. In exchange each knot carries a module doc naming its cycle, its entry points and its internal call graph, because a reader not drowning is what §11 actually protects. **Alternatives, both worse**: relax the cycle rule (spends M-separate-compilation's ordering, and 031's argument was explicitly about not doing this), or restructure the grammar to break the cycle (changing the compiler's architecture to satisfy a file-layout rule) | docs/measurements/009 § the structural finding · docs/panel/031 R6 · CLAUDE.md §11 · crates/heroes/src/syntax/ | it decides the shape of ~1,800 lines of the self-hosted compiler, and the port stops at the first knot until it is answered

- [x] panel 062 | **ANSWERED 2026-08-16, and the question split in two while it was being answered.** The item asks about *`#line` on Windows* and treats the debugger as the only instrument. It is not, and separating them is the whole answer. **The mapping is now measured on all three legs**, by `no_generated_line_points_past_or_between_the_authors_lines` — it reads the emitted C and walks the `#line` claims the way C does, so it needs no debugger and no platform. It was written for a different reason and it covers this one: it found **550 drifted C lines across 81 of 81 `run/` goldens** on the tree that was standing when this item was open, so the thing this item wanted measured was not merely unmeasured on one leg, it was **broken on all three**. **What Windows does not cover is the debugger**, and that is now recorded as option (c) — honest and free: `lldb.exe` is there and exits `0xC0000135` before running a command, a Python DLL and LLVM's installer's known gap, and the debug info itself is fine (a 7.7 MB `.pdb`, which is where Windows keeps CodeView). **The route back in is named and is deliberately not asserted**: `llvm-pdbutil` reads CodeView and, unlike `lldb.exe`, has no reason to link Python — but whether the runner carries it is a claim about the world nobody here can check, so it goes to the machine as a **question** (CLAUDE.md §1). The `Report the toolchain` step now prints whether `llvm-pdbutil` and `lldb` are on PATH, so the next CI run answers it instead of a document guessing. Was | the third CI leg, 2026-08-16 | **Is `#line` on Windows worth measuring, and with what?** `lldb_breaks_on_a_hero_line` executes design.md §2's claim on Darwin and Linux, and on Windows it executes **nothing**: `lldb.exe` is there (LLVM's, not Xcode's) but exits `0xC0000135` (`STATUS_DLL_NOT_FOUND`) with both streams empty before running a command, so the test now skips there with the reason stated. **The debug info itself is not the problem and must not be recorded as one**: `-g` writes a **7.7 MB `.pdb`** beside the binary, which is where Windows keeps CodeView — the COFF file carrying no `.debug*` section is normal. So §2's "lldb breaks on and steps through `.hero` source lines" is **unmeasured on one of three legs**, not refuted. Three ways to close it, and they cost differently: install a working lldb on the runner (a CI step, and the DLL is Python's — the LLVM installer's known gap); assert against Microsoft's debugger instead (`cdb` ships with the Windows SDK and is scriptable, but nothing in this repo speaks CodeView); or record Windows as a leg that does not cover debugging, which is honest, free, and leaves a §2 claim with two thirds of a test | crates/heroes-cli/tests/golden.rs (`lldb_starts_here`) · docs/debrief/SCHEDULED.md § the `-g` defect, fixed the same night · run 31921731797 | design.md §2 is a promise to the author about their own debugger, and the platform where it is unverified is the one platform the author cannot check by hand

- [x] panel 062 | **ANSWERED 2026-08-16 by panel 076: refused as that sitting's answer, kept alive with its conditions written down.** Five seats. It is **defeasible by any C library that installs its own `SIGSEGV` handler** — SDL, a GC, any JIT — measured at exit 139 in silence with no compile error, and §1.11 makes that the normal case rather than a corner; it **blinds ASan**, turning a 262-line symbolised report into 2 lines unless guarded by `__has_feature(address_sanitizer)`; a second fault inside it **hangs at 100% CPU**; its message **lies** when the fault is inside C; `SIGSTKSZ` and `_SC_SIGSTKSZ` compile on disjoint platforms; and Windows is a second implementation. **It returns as an unconditional REPORTER** — claiming a memory fault, never a cause — the day the three-leg CI matrix proves the classifier on Linux and Windows, which `.github/workflows/ci.yml` already runs on `workflow_dispatch`; the golden must assert **the printed line**, not exit 139, which the inverted build also produces. The historian's contradiction stays on the record: **OCaml ran this experiment for twenty years and abandoned it**, moving the check into the compiler's own prologue at OCaml 5. Was | panel 070, historian — **the shape nobody tabled, and four systems ship it** | **Turn a stack overflow into a printed message instead of a silent SEGV, at zero cost on the fast path.** Rust does exactly this in a native binary with no scheduler — `sigaltstack` plus a SIGSEGV/**and SIGBUS** handler that checks whether the faulting address is in the thread's guard-page range, then prints *"thread 'main' has overflowed its stack"* and aborts ([`library/std/src/sys/pal/unix/stack_overflow.rs`](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rust-lang/rust/master/library/std/src/sys/pal/unix/stack_overflow.rs), sourced). The JVM, OCaml and AddressSanitizer use the same mechanism; Windows has a different one (`SetThreadStackGuarantee` + a vectored handler) printing the identical message. **It reports rather than prevents**, which is why it is not a substitute for the item above — but it is the only shape that also catches deep **user** recursion, which this language will have and today has nothing for at all. Three potholes are on the record and each is a real cost: since **glibc 2.34** `SIGSTKSZ` is no longer a compile-time constant, so `static char alt[SIGSTKSZ];` **does not compile** on modern Linux and the size must come from `sysconf`; a signal stack has **no guard page of its own**, so a handler that overflows it is back to a silent SEGV (rust-lang/rust#69533, open); and **ASan installs the same handler**, so `--sanitize` and this must be reconciled rather than assumed compatible. Also owed: a decision on what the message says — Go prints the stack bounds and the pointer, which is more than Rust does, and this compiler has `#line` information Rust does not. **Panel path** (it is runtime architecture and it touches every platform), and it should be decided together with the item above rather than after it, because if it lands, the argument for an expensive prevention weakens | docs/panel/070 § the historian's row · runtime/parts/panic.c | design.md §1.12 forbids a silent crash, and this is the shape that makes *every* crash loud rather than one class of it

- [x] panels 083 and 084 | **ANSWERED 2026-08-25 — the sentence that set the trap is REPLACED; both mechanisms vetoed** (panel 090). The blind seat is the finding: given only the spec it **wrote the hanging program itself**, rated its own confidence 7/10, and named the funnel — `_` forbidden as a variant catch-all, a computed value must be used, and *"a jump is a valid arm body: it yields no value"*. Doors close until `continue` is the only blessed spelling, and `continue` skips the counter. The warden measured the damage as worse than a hang: **n=202 where a no-op gives 302, exit 0, no diagnostic**. **A `pass` keyword: vetoed twice** — §1.7 (27 exhaustive-match sites, and it reduces to nothing, so Part 5's table has no row for it) and Principle 0 (the compiler writes `x @ x` **296 times** and compiles today). **A checker diagnostic: vetoed on a measurement** — it fires on **3 of 3** `while`-enclosed `continue` arms in the compiler and **all three are correct**, 100% false positives; to fire truly it needs a termination analysis. And the hazard is narrower than I wrote: **124 of 127 `=> continue` arms are in a `for`, where the same shape terminates**. Landed: *"An arm that does nothing is a block holding `_ = 0` — `continue` is not one"*, +24, replacing the misleading sentence rather than appending to it — which answers the blind seat's vacuum objection and matches PHP's shape after it withdrew its own removal RFC. Was | **`continue` as the sanctioned arm no-op is a semantic trap inside counted loops** (M-selfhost-port, found 2026-08-17). The language refuses `_ = 0` as an arm body and the natural fillers are bare `continue`/`return`. But inside a `for`/`while` whose body ends with a counter (`at @ at + 1`), a `continue` arm SKIPS the counter — it is a loop jump, not a "nothing here". The port hit this three times in one session: an infinite loop in ir_own.hero's rewrite (a while whose index increment was skipped), and three silent position-counter desyncs in ir_phases.hero (twelve false "returns without releasing" the first time the owned checks ran on real IR). Outside a loop the compiler catches it (jump_outside_loop); inside, it is legal and wrong. Question for the author (spec/** path, panel if pursued): should arms have a true no-op form (e.g. `()` call is today's other option), or should the checker warn when a match arm's `continue` targets a loop whose body continues below the match? | the three fixes are in ir_own.hero and ir_phases.hero, commit pending

- [x] panels 083 and 084 | **ANSWERED 2026-08-18 by a FOURTH way the item had not listed, and the divergence is closed.** The three offered were: implement decimal->binary64 in Heroes over i64 arithmetic ("a real sub-project"), keep `selfhost/` float-free and gate float literals, or make both emitters pass the source text through. The fourth is the one §1.11 is built on: **C already owns a correctly-rounded decimal->binary64**, so the port asks `atof` for the VALUE and derives `%a`'s three fields with f64 arithmetic that is exact at every step — halving a normal, doubling a subnormal, `m - 1.0` for `m` in [1,2) by Sterbenz, and a mantissa scaled by a power of two under 2^52. No hand-written decimal parser, no byte reinterpretation, no language change, no runtime change, no new link (`stdlib.h` is already included). Measured over 127 programs, bootstrap C against port C: **19 differed on float spelling before, 0 after**, and the whole corpus went 26 -> 127 byte-identical with the two other repairs. Fourteen expectations in `emit_literal.hero`'s new test were read off the bootstrap's own emitted C rather than derived on paper. **And the item's premise was half false**: "Heroes has no str->f64 by design" is true of the LANGUAGE and irrelevant to the compiler, which may bind one — the same move `system()` already makes in `cli_toolchain.hero`. The sub-item nobody had filed at all: `x: f64 @ 1_0.5` printed **0.0** at exit 0 in the BOOTSTRAP, `parse::<f64>().unwrap_or(0.0)` under the comment "the parse cannot fail", and `lexer/number.rs` accepts `_` on both sides of the point. Repaired, with the fallback made loud, and a case named after it. Was | (M-selfhost-port, found 2026-08-17). The bootstrap emits f64 literals as C hex floats (`%a`, round-trip-exact, design.md §3.1) computed FROM the f64 bits. The port carries a float literal as its SPELLED TEXT, because Heroes has no str->f64 by design — so it cannot compute the bits, and would emit the decimal text instead. If the fixpoint compares bootstrap-C against port-C byte for byte (the mangler's own doc says it does), any float literal in selfhost source diverges. Three ways out, none free: (a) implement decimal->binary64->hex in Heroes over i64 arithmetic (exact, a real sub-project); (b) keep selfhost source float-free and gate float literals in the port until (a); (c) change BOTH emitters to pass the source text through (reintroduces the decimal round trip the hex rule exists to kill — likely refused). Needs an author call; (b) is the conservative default the port adopts meanwhile. | emit_literal.hero carries the divergence note

It is now `error[ffi_field_type]` at **exit 1** with the caret on the field. **The cause, precisely**: the old assertion subscripted the member, and C type-checks EVERY operand of `&&` whether or not an earlier one is false — so three ill-formed `place[0]` subscripts broke the build instead of failing the assertion. The same fact moved the sign test off a cast at panel 085 R7; this is it one position over. **The repair asks the member's ADDRESS**: `&place` is `T (*)[N]` for an array and `T *` for a scalar, both well-formed, and each association's expression is a constant over a C **type name** rather than over the member — so a scalar lands on `default: 0` and fails cleanly. **Width and sign, never identity** (panel 064): all twelve C integer types are enumerated, and the length rides inside every association as `(*)[N]`, so the separate length conjunct is gone. **Thirteen shapes attacked and all thirteen correct**: `char`, `signed char`, `unsigned char`, `short`, `int`, `long` and a `float` array all ACCEPTED — `long` as `i64` is panel 064's own point, since identity would refuse a correct binding — and a scalar, a wrong length, a wrong sign, a wrong width, a float array declared integer and a pointer array declared integer all REFUSED at exit 1. The twelve types now live once, in `emit_assert_spelling.C_INTEGER_TYPES`, read by `HERO_C_INTEGER` too: a set written twice drifts, and it drifts in the silent direction — a real C type nobody enumerated reads as *the header disagrees* when it agrees perfectly. What decides it against the exponent precedent is **who meets it**: `1e3` is what a person writes when they mean 1000 and the language has no exponents, so `exponent_literal` catches an intention; `5.` is not an intention in any language whose float literals allow a bare trailing point, because a writer who means 5.0 in Heroes writes `5.0`. The check, over `selfhost/`, `tests/harness/` and `examples/` — strings and comments stripped first, because the naive form is worthless and that was **measured before this sentence was written**: `grep -nE '[0-9]+\.($|[^0-9a-zA-Z_])'` alone reports **95** hits, every one of them prose in a comment (*"exit 0."*), and stripping only comments still reports **13**, all inside string literals (*"20. We can be heroes"*) plus two genuine UFCS calls (`5.double()`, `1.str()`) that the pattern must not claim — a falsifier that fires on the status quo cannot detect anything. The form that works excludes a following dot as well, so `0..63` inside a message does not count:<br>`for f in selfhost/*.hero tests/harness/*.hero examples/*/*.hero; do sed 's/\\"//g; s/"[^"]*"//g; s/#.*$//' "$f" | grep -nE '[0-9]+\.($|[^0-9a-zA-Z_.])' | sed "s|^|$f:|"; done`<br>**0 hits today**, and verified in both directions rather than only the quiet one: it FIRES on `x: f64 @ 5.` and stays silent on `5.to_str()`. Compare `1e3`, where panel 035 refused exponents and `exponent_literal` names the number and says what to write instead. The lexer's rule is deliberate and right (`1.str()` must stay UFCS, so a dot is a float only when a digit follows), so the question is only whether a digit run followed by `.` and **no** digit deserves its own diagnostic the way an exponent does. No capacity, no geometric growth, no fast path for a uniquely-owned array. Measured, one program per size: **25k pushes 0.9 s · 50k 3.0 s · 100k 11.7 s** — a doubling costs 4×, so building n elements is O(n²). The compiler's own cost has the matching shape: `tests/harness/main.hero` is 3,630 lines and emits in **22.0 s**, `selfhost/main.hero` is 34,812 and emits in **1,011 s** — 9.6× the source for **46×** the time (exponent ≈ 1.66). Nothing here attributes that 46× to one cause, and no `grep` was run over `selfhost/` for the idiom's count, so both are shape rather than proof. **Why it is a panel and not a fix**: `xs.push(v)` is a value — `ys = xs.push(1)` must leave `xs` untouched (spec:73, *"no aliasing exists anywhere"*) — so growing in place is sound only where the old array provably dies, which the runtime cannot see from a pointer and the **emitter** can. That makes it the runtime ABI plus the emitter's ownership contract: CLAUDE.md §4 architecture, and panel 022's *"one unshare per step of a mutated place"* is the same idea one artifact earlier. Three costs for the panel to weigh: a capacity field bumps `HERO_RUNTIME_ABI` and regenerates the seed; a second entry point (`hero_array_push_owned`) is emitter surface and a new way to be wrong; any in-place path must survive `--sanitize` plus `hero_runtime_check_leaks()`, where an aliasing mistake is a use-after-free rather than a wrong answer. **My recommendation: convene it, and soon** — not for elegance but because CLAUDE.md §13's second clause is the author's own (*"performance is not a goal but must not be a ceiling"*), and a 20-minute test loop is what makes a verification stop being run. - [x] panel 087 | **CLOSED 2026-08-24, and the item's own claim was FALSE** (panel 089, compiler-engineer, reproduced by the coordinator the hard way — by writing the defect into the first draft of the repair and watching it fire). The claim was that *"the whole class of 'ask C whether this pointer is safe' is closed"*. What is closed is asking **C** whether a pointer is **NULL** — and NULL is the one question the language already answers for itself, `c == nullptr`, so it never has to cross. Everything else about a non-null pointer is askable. The first draft of `validated` put the null test in C and died exactly as this item predicted (`panic: a null cstr was passed to a C function`, exit 134); moving that one line into Heroes fixed it, and `library_source.hero` now carries the measurement in its own comment so nobody repeats the trip. **`hero_cstr_nonnull` is untouched and needs no exemption** — an exemption would have been a hole in §12's guard for a problem that does not exist. So the whole class of *"ask C whether this pointer is safe"* is closed, and §1.12 says the boundary must be **complete**. The four EXTRA failures a clean worktree shows are the same cause with nothing in `build/` at all.

- [x] defect — **FIXED 2026-08-16, M-selfhost-port step 1, and it was two defects rather than one.** `-g` is in `FLAGS` now, so every build carries DWARF. But that alone fixed nothing: the generated unit's object was a clang **temporary**, deleted after linking, and on Darwin the DWARF *lives* in the object — the binary carries only a debug map (`N_OSO`) pointing at it. So the debug info evaporated at the end of every build, `-g` or no `-g`, and the breakpoint stayed `pending`. `Toolchain::link` now compiles the unit to an object beside its `.c` in the same content-addressed directory and links that. **The claim is executed by an artifact at last** (`lldb_breaks_on_a_hero_line`, golden.rs): `breakpoint set --file arithmetic.hero --line N` resolves, fires, and lldb reports the `.hero` line. Measured cost: binary size unchanged (the DWARF is in the object), the kept object ~8 KB beside an ~8 KB `.c`, one extra clang invocation per build. §2's known ceiling stands — `frame variable` prints mangled C locals. Was | **`-g` reaches clang only under `--sanitize`** (`toolchain.rs:203`, the file's one occurrence), so an ordinary build carries no DWARF — while design.md §2 ("line-level debugging works: lldb breaks on and steps through `.hero` source lines") and §3.1 ("Debug info. `#line` directives map generated C back to `.hero` lines") both state the opposite. The `#line` machinery is tested; the claim about lldb is tested by nothing. Group `constant`s and `record`s stay reachable qualified, deliberately: their C is compiler-written on both sides, so the header is only needed where they are declared. Row 1 (panel 030 R2, panel 033) says an `extern` is **never** callable across a module boundary and that a qualified call to one is a type error, with the route being a Heroes function in the declaring module. - [x] panel 057, the brief that convened it | **The brief for the path pair, kept because the sitting it convened REFUSED the proposal** (panel 057, three vetoes, 2026-08-15). `/decide` could not close it for a jurisdictional reason rather than a hesitation, and the reasoning below is why: two new built-in names plus spec tokens is surface, so CLAUDE.md §4 makes it a `/panel` path, and the soundness lane does not fit because the change costs spec tokens. Verified 2026-08-26: neither `path_join` nor `path_parent` exists in `selfhost/` or the spec. The brief verbatim:

  **This is the one item of the eight `/decide` took on 2026-08-15 that `/decide`
  could not close, and the reason is jurisdictional rather than a hesitation.** Two
  new built-in names plus spec tokens is surface, so CLAUDE.md §4 makes it a `/panel`
  path, and the soundness lane does not fit because the change costs spec tokens. The
  skill's own rule is to say so and stop. **The proposal is already instructed, so the
  sitting can be convened whenever there is time to wait for it:**

  > **Adopt `path_join(dir: str, name: str) -> str` and `path_parent(p: str) -> str?`,
  > neither of which normalises.**
  >
  > - **`path_parent` is the one with the §1.0 argument.** The port's module loader
  >   must take a path the author typed and find its directory part;
  >   `modules/mod.rs`'s `directory_text` does `path.rfind(['/', '\\'])`, looking for
  >   **either** separator because the author may have typed a Windows one. In Heroes
  >   that is a backwards loop over a string looking for two characters — the shape a
  >   built-in exists to remove.
  > - **`path_join` has no §1.0 argument and comes along anyway.** Verified: no corpus
  >   program builds a path, and `dir + "/" + name` is already correct on all three
  >   platforms, so it buys **discoverability, not correctness**. It enters as the
  >   inverse of a function that does have the argument, or not at all.
  > - **`path_parent` returns `str?`.** Absent when there is no separator. Returning
  >   `"."` would be inventing a meaning; `str?` makes the caller handle it and is the
  >   language's own idiom (§4.6, absence as a variant).
  > - **`path_join` inserts one `/` unless `dir` already ends in `/` or `\`** — a fact
  >   about the value in hand (§11), not a decision about a path's meaning.
  > - **Neither normalises, and that is the load-bearing half.** The runtime is
  >   portable today *because it does nothing to a path*: `runtime/parts/os.c` has no
  >   `strcat`, no separator literal, no normalisation, and the string reaches `fopen`
  >   verbatim. A `path_join` that rewrote `\` to `/` or collapsed `..` would be the
  >   first place this project decides what a path **means**, and that is a larger
  >   step than the name suggests — it is also the step that has produced security
  >   defects in every language that took it eagerly.
  >
  > **What the sitting must price**: the spec tokens for two names against §1.6's
  > headroom (888 today), and whether `path_parent` alone is the honest §1.0 purchase
  > with `path_join` refused under Principle 0's *neither → it waits*.

  Was | author instruction, 2026-08-14 | **A runtime function that joins two path pieces — and the honest question is whether joining is the half that is missing.** Verified before writing this: **no program in the corpus builds a path today**, `runtime/parts/os.c` does nothing to one (no `strcat`, no separator literal, no normalisation — the string reaches `fopen` verbatim), and `/` works on all three platforms because Windows accepts it in every filesystem API. `examples/adventure/main.hero` proves it on the Windows leg with a literal `examples/adventure/walkthrough.txt`. So a program that wants to join **can** today: `dir + "/" + name` is correct everywhere, and a doubled separator from a trailing `/` is harmless on all three. **Which means `path_join` buys discoverability, not correctness — and that is not a Principle 0 argument.** What *is* one is the other half. The port's module loader must take a path the **author typed** on the command line and find its directory part: `crates/heroes/src/modules/mod.rs`'s `directory_text` does `path.rfind(['/', '\\'])`, looking for **either** separator, because the author may have typed a Windows one. In Heroes today that is writable — `chars`, `slice`, `len` — and it is a loop over the string backwards looking for two characters, which is the shape a built-in exists to remove. **So the three options are not join-or-nothing**: (a) `path_join(dir, name)` alone, which is the asked-for thing and the half the port does not need; (b) **the pair** — `path_join` and `path_parent` — where the second is the one with the §1.0 argument and the first comes along because a program that can take a path apart will want to put one together; (c) neither, and the port writes the backwards loop once, in the file that already knows why (`modules/`), with `directory_text`'s own comment carried over. **A fourth thing to decide with it**: whether either function normalises. The runtime's portability today is that it does **nothing**, written down as a claim that can die — a `path_join` that rewrote `\` to `/`, or collapsed `..`, would be the first place this project makes a decision about a path's *meaning*, and that is a larger step than the name suggests | runtime/parts/os.c · crates/heroes/src/modules/mod.rs (`directory_text`) · crates/heroes/src/library/source.hero · spec § Strings, arrays, maps | the runtime is portable because it does nothing to a path, and the first function that does something is where that stops being true

  | docs/panel/057-the-path-pair.md · CLAUDE.md §4 | a refused proposal is worth keeping in the shape it was refused in, or the next sitting re-derives it

- [x] M-selfhost-port, the CLI department | **CONFIRMED BY CONDUCT — the decision shipped and is load-bearing.** The prose below ended *"Veto or confirm"*; nobody vetoed and `system()` is how the compiler reaches clang today (`selfhost/cli_toolchain.hero:14`, and since M-separate-compilation step 5 every call routes through `cli_shell.shell` because an `extern` may not cross a module boundary). The decision verbatim:

  The language has read_file/write_file/args/exit and no subprocess form. The
  port's CLI needs to invoke clang and run the produced binary. The decision
  taken (revocable): bind C's own `system()` via the language's FFI —
  `extern "stdlib.h" function system(command: cstr) -> i64` — with clang's
  stderr redirected to a file under build/ and read back with read_file, so
  the ffi error mapping keeps working. This is §1.11 eating its own cooking:
  the compiler written in Heroes reaches its toolchain the way any Heroes
  program reaches C. The alternative (a runtime entry point wrapping
  posix_spawn with argv separation) is safer against quoting but grows the
  runtime; system() + careful quoting of the few paths we pass is v1. Veto or
  confirm.

  | selfhost/cli_toolchain.hero · selfhost/cli_shell.hero | the compiler written in Heroes reaches its toolchain the way any Heroes program reaches C, which is §1.11 eating its own cooking

- [x] M-selfhost-port, 2026-08-17 | **THE MEASUREMENT THAT ROUTED A MILESTONE, kept whole.** It is CLAUDE.md §13's named case — performance is not a goal *and not a licence* — and the numbers below are what made the fixpoint's cost the author's to route rather than an assistant's optimisation instinct. The measurement verbatim:

  The port compiles small programs instantly and its own 34,512 lines not at
  all within 20 minutes. Measured with the -O0 selfhost binary, `check` only
  (frontend, no emit), wall clock:

  | input | modules pulled in | time |
  |---|---|---|
  | `token.hero` (193 lines) | 2 | 0s |
  | `parse.hero` | the lexer + parser subtree | 24s |
  | `checker.hero` | the frontend subtree | 152s |
  | `emit.hero` | + the whole backend | >600s (killed) |
  | `main.hero` (everything) | 143 | >1200s (killed) |

  Superlinear, and the shape says where to look before any guess: every
  `for … in xs` over a growing `[T]` in this port copies (COW value
  semantics, §4.20), and several walks are O(n) scans inside O(n) loops —
  `out_edges` in check_sized rescans every edge per node, `resolve_state`'s
  `top_visible` scans the table, `emit_ctype`'s tables are maps keyed by
  declaration. None of that is a language defect; it is the port written the
  way the Rust reads rather than the way this language costs.

  **This is CLAUDE.md §13's named case, and it is the author's to route.**
  Performance is not a goal *and not a licence*: where a cost stops a program
  the closure list needs from running at all, that is §1.0 compiler-need and
  it goes to the panel rather than to an assistant's optimisation instinct.
  The fixpoint (M-selfhost-fixpoint) is the v1 finish line and it cannot be
  reached at these numbers.

  **Measurement (a) is now done, and it moves the numbers without moving the
  question.** The same inputs, the selfhost binary built at -O2 instead of
  -O0 (4.1 MB against 6.3 MB):

  | input | -O0 | -O2 | ratio |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `token.hero` | 0s | 1s | — (both noise) |
  | `parse.hero` | 24s | 8s | 3.0x |
  | `checker.hero` | 152s | 54s | 2.8x |

  A constant factor of ~2.8x against a superlinear curve: it buys one step
  along the curve, not the shape of it. Whether it is enough for all 143
  modules is being measured as this entry is written and the answer belongs
  under it.

  **Measurement (b) is still owed, and it is the one a sitting needs**: a
  profile naming the top three call sites, so the panel is briefed with where
  the time goes rather than with the shape argument above — which is a
  reading of the code, not a measurement of it (§1's rule about an inference
  presented as a measurement). It needs an instrument this project does not
  have on the surface, and adding one is itself a §10 stopping-rule question:
  `--profile` would be a flag nothing in the fixpoint invocation types.
  The cheap alternative that needs no surface at all is `sample` (macOS) or
  `perf` (Linux) against the running binary, which is the machine's
  instrument rather than this compiler's.

  | CLAUDE.md §13 · docs/journal/021-selfhost-port.md | a number nobody wrote down is a number the next sitting invents
