Was:

  M-separate-compilation step 6, **the work is on disk and uncommitted, and this is why** (2026-08-25) | **The per-module emitter works and is too slow to commit, with the cause measured and the repair named.** What runs today (uncommitted: `selfhost/emit_unit.hero`, `selfhost/cli_units.hero`, `selfhost/emit_synth.hero`'s `Wanted`, the `HERO_TU_LOCAL`/`HERO_TU_QUIET` threading, the `cli_verbs` per-module branch): the compiler builds ITSELF as 157 translation units, links, and the binary runs; `heroes run examples/calculator/main.hero` is correct through 5 TUs; the cache is selective (one module edited → one TU recompiled, measured); `emission` 297/297, `warnings` 111/111 cold AND warm (the warm case needed the replay of a TU's saved warnings — a cache that swallows a warning is a cache that falsifies §7), `surface` 85/85. **The number that blocks it**: fused self-build on a clean cache **203 s** (3:23, re-measured today — the earlier 188 s reference still holds); per-module **>900 s and still running when it was killed**. **The cause is NOT the emitted text** — that was the first hypothesis, and the trim it produced is real and worth keeping (an unfiltered TU was 19,193 lines of which 87% belonged to other modules; `emit_synth.Wanted` now filters typedefs, per-type functions and descriptors by the TU's own reach). The cause is the **per-TU setup**: `unit()` rebuilds `new_names` + `with_options` + `with_functions` for every module — three whole-ARENA walks × 157 — and `members()` calls `emit.shown_fused(p, s)` per module, another whole-program walk × 157. **The repair is a hoist, not a redesign**: those tables are whole-program and, since panel 093 R6's content names, identical for every TU, so they are computed ONCE by the caller and passed in; `shown_fused` likewise. **Two measurement lessons paid for today and worth more than the code**: a `build/` directory left to grow to **31 GB / 49,554 entries** made every timing meaningless (the first per-module reading, 484 s, was taken against a clean-disk baseline — a comparison rigged by the measurer, caught by `du`), and a baseline older than the tree it is compared against is not a baseline | selfhost/emit_unit.hero · selfhost/cli_units.hero · docs/panel/093 | the architecture is proven and the cost is in one loop; committing before the hoist would land a slowdown the author's own rule forbids

- [x] **The spec never says what `print` guarantees a float, and the question is real even though this item's witness is dead** | **REWRITTEN 2026-08-28: the premise was stale by three weeks and the Java example proves the opposite of what it was cited for.** What the item claimed: the runtime renders `5e-324` as `4.94065645841247e-324`, so a reader who infers *shortest* from the spec is misled. **Measured against the live runtime** (`hero_f64_to_str`, compiled directly): `5e-324` renders **`5e-324`**, `1e-323` renders **`1e-323`** — both matching Python. `DESIGN-LOG:100`, 2026-08-05, says exactly this and says why: gnulib's `ftoastr` subnormal branch landed, *"and the word shortest is struck"*, because the spec-warden's counterexample had dissolved while the wording correction stood. The item was reading `runtime/parts/f64.c:26`'s comment, which still carries the pre-branch example. **AND THE JAVA CASE CUTS THE OTHER WAY**: `1.9999999999999998e23`, the value Java shipped for eighteen years, Heroes prints **`2e+23`** — the shortest form that round-trips, i.e. the right answer. The item cited it as evidence of a gap. **A THIRD CORRECTION, found while verifying**: the witness is **not writable in Heroes at all** — `5.0e-324` is `error[exponent_literal]: there are no exponents`, so it is a fact about the C runtime and not a program a judge can compile. Any brief must say so or a seat will try. **WHAT IS ACTUALLY OPEN, and it is smaller and cleaner**: `runtime/parts/f64.c:26` guarantees ROUND-TRIP-EXACTNESS through a `%.*g` ladder, and `grep -i 'round.trip|shortest'` over the spec returns **nothing**. The spec says only *"A float prints a point or exponent (`1.0`, `1e-06`)"*. A ladder is not a shortest algorithm even when it usually agrees with one, so the guarantee a program may rely on is unstated. Does the spec owe one sentence naming round-trip? - [x] **Two harness runs at once CORRUPT the blessed emission traces, and one of them was writing them** | **Found by doing it, 2026-08-28, and it is the most serious thing found that day.** `UPDATE_EMISSION=1 heroes run tests/harness/main.hero -- <compiler> emission` was started while an ordinary net run was still finishing in the background. Both use `build/harness` as their scratch (`tests/harness/main.hero`'s `SCRATCH`), so their captured streams collided — and the blessing wrote the collision to disk. Measured damage: **three traces rewritten with a program's stdout spliced into the C**, including a mangled first line — `tests/emission/run-ffi-cstr.c` began `s 0.0.1 from tests/golden/run/ffi-cstr.hero. Do not edit. */` with `20 30`, `2 5` and `cd abcdef!` inserted above it. Reverted with `git checkout`; the same command run alone afterwards was clean, **299 passed 0 failed, one trace added and no other touched**, so this is a RACE and not a systematic defect. **Why it matters more than an ordinary race**: `suite_emission.hero`'s own doc says a blessed file *"is not one compiler's opinion: it is what two independent implementations agreed on, byte for byte"* — the bootstrap is archived, so those bytes **cannot be regenerated from their original source of truth**. A corrupted trace that got committed would replace a two-compiler agreement with garbage, and the suite would then be green about it. **Three ways out**: (a) the scratch directory carries the process id, so two runs cannot share it — smallest and it removes the class; (b) a lock file under `build/`, and the second run refuses with a diagnostic naming the first; (c) `UPDATE_EMISSION=1` refuses to write when the scratch already holds another run's files. **The default while it waits**: the corruption is silent, `git status` is the only thing that showed it, and nothing in the harness would have caught a committed one | tests/harness/main.hero (`SCRATCH`) · tests/harness/suite_emission.hero · tests/harness/shell.hero (`run`) | the emission traces are the one instrument in this repository whose source of truth no longer exists, and a race can overwrite them while reporting success

Do not edit. */`), so that no corrupted trace can be blessed whatever corrupted it. The first half removes the race; the second makes the instrument refuse garbage from any cause. A lock file alone (b) was declined: it protects against the one collision that was seen. Scheduled in `docs/work/SCHEDULED.md`.

- [x] **A Heroes program that recurses deeply enough dies at exit 139 with no message, and §1.12 makes that a goal's failure rather than a limitation** | **Found 2026-08-28 while checking the site's own claims before publication, and the site was claiming the opposite.** `zen.html`'s line 17 says *"Nothing crashes and nothing leaks"*, `index.html` headed a section *"A small surface, no collector, and no crash"*, and `why.html` said *"one hole remains, and it is declared"* naming only the C boundary. **Measured on the live compiler**, an ordinary recursive function (`function down(n: i64) -> i64` returning `1 + down(n - 1)`): 500,000 frames is **exit 0**, 1,000,000 frames is **exit 139**, SIGSEGV, with no output and nothing on stderr. Nothing in the language, the runtime or the emitted C sees it. **THE DIAGNOSIS ALREADY EXISTS AND IS GOOD** — under `--sanitize` the same program is **exit 134** with `AddressSanitizer: stack-overflow` and, decisively, `#0 h_d_down d.hero:1` plus `SUMMARY: ... stack-overflow d.hero:1 in h_d_down`: the sanitizer names the **`.hero` line**, so the information a diagnostic would need is already reachable. What is missing is the default build. **Why this is a decision and not just work**: §1.12 makes not crashing a GOAL of the language and CLAUDE.md §12 gives it the tie-break over elegance, tokens, ergonomics and speed, so the question is which of three shapes the goal buys, and they cost differently. (a) A **guard page plus a handler** that turns the fault into `panic: stack exhausted` at the abort path every other runtime failure uses, which is the only option that keeps the message on the author's own program without touching what is emitted. (b) A **depth counter** in the prologue of every emitted function, which is honest, portable and a per-call cost on every program including those that never recurse, so it is the one option §13's performance line has an opinion about. (c) **Declare it**, as the FFI hole is declared, and let the guarantee read *"two holes remain"* — which is what the SITE now does, because a published promise that a five-line program falsifies is worse than a published limit. (c) is already taken for the copy and does not settle the language. **The default while it waits**: the site declares the hole, the compiler stays silent about it, and the only way a program's author learns which function ran out is to re-run under `--sanitize`. **Not measured and worth measuring before the sitting**: whether the same program is 139 on Linux, where the stack and the guard page are the kernel's rather than Darwin's | spec:1 (`§1.12`, the goal) · CLAUDE.md §12, §13 · runtime/runtime.c (`hero_panic`, the abort path a message would take) · site/src/html/why.html, zen.html (where the claim was, and what it says now) | a language whose stated goal is that programs do not crash, crashing without a word on a five-line program, is the one defect its own showcase site cannot survive being asked about

  **CLOSED 2026-09-03** (author instruction 2026-09-03, *"ratifica tutto e poi dimmi cosa rimane da fare"*, then *"aspetta scegli le soluzioni più robuste e complete rispetto a quelle più economiche"* — the second sentence chose between the options wherever the first recommendation had been the cheaper one). **Decision: (a), in its complete form** — a guard-page handler in the runtime (alternate signal stack on POSIX, a vectored exception handler on Windows) that turns the fault into `panic: stack exhausted` on the abort path every other runtime failure uses, exit 134, zero cost per call, PLUS symbolisation of the faulting frame (`backtrace`/`dladdr` on POSIX, `CaptureStackBackTrace`/`SymFromAddr` on Windows; the mangler is deterministic, so `h_d_down` reads back as `d.down`) so the message names the Heroes function without `--sanitize`. The soundness lane sits first (compiler-engineer + ffi-pragmatist: no surface, no spec token, no diagnostic class — a runtime panic like the others) and prices (b), the emitted depth counter, as the fallback where symbolisation cannot name the frame. (c) stays for the site's copy only until the handler lands. **Re-measured this session on the seed of 2026-09-02 17:52**: Mac 50,000 frames exit 0, 100,000 exit 139 — the item's 500,000 was measured on a smaller frame; Linux (the clang 22 image) 100,000 and 500,000 both exit 139; `--sanitize` names `d1000000.hero:2 in h_d1000000_down`; `grep sigaltstack|SIGSEGV|sigaction` over `runtime/` returns nothing, so the handler starts from zero. Scheduled in `docs/work/SCHEDULED.md`.

- [x] **`@` ON A BY-VALUE PARAMETER CORRUPTS THE HEAP, and the spec already forbids the program** | **Found by panel 102's compiler-engineer on its way to something else, minimised to 14 lines, and reproduced independently by the coordinator the same hour.** `spec:90` says *"only a declared `@` name can be mutated"*; `function reader(b: Bag)` declares `b` immutable; `add(@b, "x")` mutates it; the compiler **accepts it**. Measured on the live seed compiler at `d08062f`: the program prints `2` and then `panic: 1 heap blocks still live at exit (a missing decref) — this is a compiler bug`, **exit 134**; a two-call variant is **exit 133 with no output at all**; and under `--sanitize` it is **`AddressSanitizer: heap-use-after-free`**, READ of size 8 in `hero_array_decref` at `array.c:77`, freed from `array.c:84` in the same `main`. **The cause is located rather than guessed**: `selfhost/resolve/walk.hero:231`'s `write_root` is the check that refuses a write to an immutable binding, and `grep` shows it has **exactly one caller**, `place()` at `:206`, which handles a write STATEMENT. A call argument's `@` marker never reaches it — the checker's `marker_mismatch` at `check_walk.hero:1491` only asks whether the marker matches the PARAMETER's marker, never whether the argument is a mutable binding. So the missing check is one hop, in the pass that already owns the rule. **Why this is a decision and not just work**: §1.12 makes memory safety a GOAL and CLAUDE.md §12 gives it the tie-break over everything, so it is done — but CLAUDE.md §1 says a repair is attacked at the shapes NEXT to the one that provoked it, and here they are named rather than discovered later: `@` on a field (`add(@r.items, …)`), on an index, on a map value, on a nested record, on a `for` variable, on a `match` payload, and inside a generic. Each needs its own case before the commit. **MEASURED 2026-09-03, eight shapes on the seed of 2026-09-02 17:52, and seven of the eight pass `heroes check` at exit 0**: a `=` local (`bag = Bag(…)` then `add(@bag, "x")`) runs to exit 0 and prints `2` — the immutable was mutated in silence; a field (`add_items(@b.items, "x")` through a by-value `b`) is exit 134 with the leak panic; an index (`add(@bs[0], "x")`) is **exit 138**, SIGBUS; a nested record (`add(@x.bag, "x")`) is exit 134; a `for` variable (`for b in bs` then `add(@b, "x")`) prints `2 2` at exit 0; a `match` payload (`.some s => add(@s.bag, "x")`) prints `2` at exit 0; a generic (`poke<T>(@x: T)` called as `poke(@v)` on a by-value `v: T`) is accepted and runs; and the ONE refusal, `add(@m["k"], "x")`, is refused as `type_mismatch` because `m["k"]` is a `V?` — the right verdict for the wrong reason, so it proves nothing about the rule. Three corruptions, three silent mutations of an immutable, one accepted no-op, zero `write_root` refusals: the missing check is one rule with eight witnesses, and the eight programs are the step's golden cases. **The default while it waits**: a program the spec forbids builds clean and corrupts memory, and nothing in the three suites sees it — which is its own finding, since `heroes mutate`'s corpus and `--sanitize` are how §12 says robustness is measured rather than asserted | spec:90 · selfhost/resolve/walk.hero:206, :231 (`place`, `write_root`) · selfhost/check/walk.hero:1491 (`marker_mismatch`) · docs/panel/102 § the engineer's row | §1.12 says a Heroes program must not corrupt memory, and this is fourteen lines that do

  **CLOSED 2026-09-03** (author instruction 2026-09-03, *"ratifica tutto e poi dimmi cosa rimane da fare"*, then *"aspetta scegli le soluzioni più robuste e complete rispetto a quelle più economiche"* — the second sentence chose between the options wherever the first recommendation had been the cheaper one). **Decision: fix it as a step, and it is the first step of what remains**, because CLAUDE.md §12 ranks memory safety above everything else on the list. One rule, in the pass that owns it: `write_root` (`selfhost/resolve/walk.hero:231`) is reached from every call-argument `@`, not only from a write statement, so the eight measured shapes are refused with the existing immutable-write diagnostic at the argument's span. Eight golden cases from the eight programs in this item (seven accepted today, three of them corrupting memory), plus a `fixedbugs-` case carrying symptom, cause and date; the map case keeps its `type_mismatch` and gains nothing, because `m["k"]` is a `V?` and that refusal is right. Panel 101 R4 had already ruled this class (*"a certain fix that does not compile is the one thing a fix may never be"*) and it was live again one branch over, on the FFI's most natural spelling. **Panel 102's (A) does NOT close the reachable path, and the sentence that stood here said it did** (corrected 2026-09-03, measured on the seed of 2026-09-02 17:52, which carries (A): `use window` beside `window: ptr @ nullptr` and `_ = window.destroy()` gives **two** diagnostics at exit 1, `shadowed_binding` AND `needs_qualifying`, because (A) reports and then BINDS the local, so the dot still resolves through a `ptr` with no fields and falls to the module path; and `heroes check --apply` on it is **exit 0** writing `window.window.destroy()` — the fix reproduces itself today, on the FFI's most natural spelling).
